l3
4. The present challenge IS regard the conduct of the Mains
examinations by the Telangana Public Service Commission vide
Notification No.02l2024, dated 19.02.2024 for recruitment to Group-l
services (General Recruitment) for 563 posts. On 09.06.2024, preliminary
examination was conducted, the results of which were declared on
07.A7.2024. The Mains examination was conducted from 21.10.2024 to
27.10.2024 in 46 centres. Evaluation of the said examination was done
from 0l .11.2024 to 3l .01.2025. The results of the said examination were
declared on 30.03.2025. Alleging that there are inconsistencies ln
conducting the Mains examination and in the method of evaluation, the
writ petitioners, who are unsuccessful candidates, filed the W.P.No.l1439
of 2025 and batch. The leamed writ court by a common order dated
09.09.2025 disposed of the said writ petitions with the directions referred
to hereinunder. It is appropriate to indicate the altemative directions which
the learned writ court has granted by allowing the writ petitions at paras
369 to 372 which are as under:
uConclusion:
369- After analysingttle submissions of all the counsels, case laws,
and the material available on record, this Court feels that the
Commission, while condtrcting the Group-I Mains Examination had
not maintained transparency, integrity, acted in biased manner and
deviated their own Rules.
370. Due to the Commission's negligence and ineffrcierrcy, the
affected individuals are the unanployee youth of Telangana State.
The candidates have spent a substantial anrount on coaching classes,

t4
stJdying l0 to 12 hours a day and some candidates had resigned their
jobs for the sake of preparation for this exam. The Commission failed in both procedural aspects and evaluation method, which is
inrpermissible.
312. ln view of the foregoing discussions and also taking inro
consideration the facts and circumstances mentioned above, these writ
petitions are disposed of with the following directions:-
3i'1. Although, there have been two instances of cancellation of
Group-l examination, the Commission has not leamr from its
previous experience.
(a) The Final Marks List dated 10.03.2025 as well as General
Ranking List (GRL) dated 30.03.2025 are hereby sa aside.
(b) The respondent authorities arc directed to rc-evaluate all rhe
an.;wer scripts of the Gmup-l Mains examination by applying the
moderation method in terms of &njay Singh and Anr- Ys. IJ.p.pubtic
Se,'vice Commission, Allahabad (2007) 3 SCC 72O), manually and
an rounce the results- Based upon such r€sults, the respondents
aurhorities are directed !o take st€ps for filling up ofsa l 563 posts.
(oTrrERwrsE)
(c) This Coun is inclined to cancel the Group-I Mains examination.
Th': respondent authoritics are directed to reonduct the GroupJ
Ma ins examination in Notification No .0Am2+ dated 19-O2.2024, for
those who have succeeded ip thg pr-elios oramination.
d) The above said exercise should be corpleted within a period of
eigrt (08) months from the dare of receipt ofa copy of this common
order."
5. For the convenience sake, the learned counsels for the parties have
broadly rt:lied upon the pleadings and annexures of W.A.No.1066 of 2025.
The entire pleadings have been compiled in seven volumes. Therefore, this
Court wc'uld rely upon the pleadings and supporting documents in
W.A.No.l066 of 2025.

l5
Impugned Judgment:
6. The writ petitioners assailed the results of the Group-l examination
on a number of grounds. The learned writ court divided the entire issues
into two parts, they are I - Procedural Aspects and II - Method of
evaluation. The issues identified under these heads ulre as under:
*l ) PROCEDLTRAL ASPECTS:-
a) Issuing of two hall tickets forthe GrcupI Examination.
b) Allocation of examination centres'in a biased rnanner.
c) lnconsistency about the total number of candidates, who
appeared for the rnain examination
d) Multiple evaluation of answer scripts.
e) Publication of provisionaUtotal marks list.
f) Disclosing of unqualified candidate's hall ticket numbers
in the Mains final marks list.
g) lncreasing vacancies by bdding,tie*l vacarrcies.
2) ivlETIIOD OF EVALUAfiON:-
a) The selection of the enaluators.
b) The procedure of evaluation.
c) Telugu medium candidates were neglected.
d) Highest qualified candidates in specific cextres.
e) Commission awarded the sarne marks to certain candidates-"
7. Be it indicated here that the leanred writ court answered issues
(a) to (f) under the head 'Procedural Aspects' in favour of the writ
petitioners, whereas issue (g) relates to increasing vacancies by adding
new vacancies was answered in favour of the Commission. The learned
writ court answered the issues (a) to (e) under the head 'Method of
Evaluation' against the Commission. Based on these findings, the learned
writ court in its conclusion at paras 369 to 372 held that the Commission

r6
had not maintained transparency, integrity, acted in biased manner and
deviated from their own rules. The learned writ court set aside the Final
Marks [,ist dated 10.03.202s as well as General Ranking List dated
30.03.2025. The learned writ court proceeded to issue alternative
directions, i.e., the respondent authorities were directed to re-evaluate all
the ansu'er scripts of the Group-l Mains examination by applying the
moderati,rn method in terms of Sanjay Singh (supra), rnanually and
announct) the results. Based upon such results, the respondent au.thorities
are direcled to take steps for filling up of said 563 posts. (oTHERwIsE)
This Court was inclined to cancel the croup-l Mains examination. The
respondent authorities were directed to re-conduct the Group-I Mains
examinati.on in NotiFrcation No.02l2024 dated 19.02.2024, for,tliose who
'have sucr:eeded in the prelims examination. These directions are under
challenge by the Commission and the successful candidates by filing these
appeals except w.A.No.1470 of 2025. Be it indicated here that the writ
petitionen; had not impleaded any o[ the successfirl candidates. The
successful. candidates were impleaded at their instance during the course of
the proceedings of the case.

t7
Submissions of Mr. A.Sudarshan .learned Advocate General on
behalf of the Telangana Public Service Commission/
Appellan ts/Successfu! teaudidatgs:
(l) Procedural Aspects:
(a) Issuing of trvo hall tickets for the Group-I Examination:
8. Mr. A.Sudarshan Reddy, learnd Advocate General has submitted
that after the Prelims examination results were declared on 07.07.2024, a
Web Note was issued on 16.08.2024 giving instructions to the candidates
selected for the Mains examinatiorr to be held from 21.10.2024 to
27.10.2024. It is clearly stated therein that a new hall ticket number would
be issued to the candidates appearing for the Mains examination. The hall
ticket flor the Mains examination (Conventional Type) reflected the
Prelims exarnination hall ticket number. Therefore, no prejudice was
caused to the candidates. He has further pointed out that the High Court for
the State of Telangana also issued two separate hall tickets in conducting
the examination for appointment to the porgts of Civil Judge (Junior
Division) for Screening Test (Computer Based Test) and Hall Ticket for
the Written Examination. Therefore, there was nothing wrong in issuing
two hall tickets for administrative convenience. Two hall tickets were
given for providing a continuous sequence for hall tickets which would be
convenient for printing OMR sheets accompanying the answer scripts,

I {,1
distributing them across examination cenrres and also in the examination
halls. fire writ petitioners failed to demonstrate any prejudice caused to
them in issuance of two hall tickers for prelims examination and Mains
examinar:ion. The procedure followed by rhe Commission was transparetrt
and not arbitrary.
(b) Nlocation of examination centres in a biased menner:
8.1. It is submitted that initially 45 centres were found to be suflicient
for the Mains examination. However. upon physical verification during the
first week of Octobeq 2024, ir was noriccd rhar ar Centre No.l9, the
ground floor is at an elevation making ir inaccessible to Persons with
Disabilig, @wD) candidates, 87 such PwD candidares originally allotted to
Centre Nb.l9 were relocated ro Cenrre No.2 I (40 candidates), Centre
No.39 (41 candidates) and Centre No.36 (4 candidates). Since additional
space warl allocated in Test Centres for PwD candidates (appearing with
scribes), there was a requirement to constitute a new Test Centre to
accommodate the candidates. Therefore, one Test Centre was added,
making tbe Test Centres to 46.
8.2. Leamed Advocate Gencral has pointed out that there are no
separate toilets for men in Koti Wonren's College. When the Union public
Service C:ommission (UPSC) conducred examination at that particular
centre acsommodating both men and rvornen, there was a lot of

t9
inconvenience for the men. The candidates had to queue up outside the
toilets. It was reported in newspapers. Taking this into consideration, the
Commission after deliberation, allocated only women for the Test Centre
Nos.l8 and 19 sinrated in Koti Women's College. He has submitted that
all this process was conducted before issuing the hall tickets and no
prejudice is caused to the candidates appearing for the Mains examination
for increasing the test cenffes from 45 to 46,
(c) Inconsistency about the total number of candidates, who appeared
for the main examination:
8.3. Learned Advocate General has drawn the attention of this Court to
the Web Note of 'statistical Summary of Attendance Pertaining to Mains
Examinations held from 21.10.2024 AN to 27.10.2024 AN' dated
27.10.2024 issued by the Secretary of the Commission. He has submitted
that in column (iv) in the Note below the Table specifically rnentioned that
'the figures pertaining to the number of candidates who appeared for the
Mains Exams are collected from the Chief Superintendents of the exam
centreson the go when the examinatton was in progress and are subject to
minor variation. f any. ' He further submitted that on 27.10.2024, the last
day of the Mains examination, it was tentatively arrived at21,093. After
reconciliation, the said figure was revised to 2l,l l0 as 17 candidates who

of answer scripts:
8.4. Le-;rrned Advocate General has subrnitted that all the answer scriBts
in the Mains examination were evaluated by two different evaluators.
However, in case there was a difference of more than l5% marks awarded
by two evaluators, a third evaluation was carried out. The process of
evaluatio.n. of each evaluator was closely monitored by the Chief Examincr
tb ensure 'lhat the evaluation is strictly irnplemented in terms of uniformity
and fairnr;ss. A system of scrutiny was undertaken after each evaluation to
veriff whether marks are awarded to each question answered by the

2t
candidate and whether the totals are correctly done. For all the languages,
adequate number of teaching faculty had been engaged for evaluation. The
Commission thoroughly identified and accommodated the subject experts
across the country and did not compromise with the quality of expertise.
All the evaluators engaged in the assessment of answer scripts were duly
trained by the Commission.
8.5. Learned Advocate Ceneral has further subrnitted that the allegation
that there is no separate bar code for the third evaluation slip, while the
first and second evaluation (on the first page of the Answer Booklet) carry
unique bar codes, which render the third evaluation subject to
manipulation is not acceptable. He has submitted that since all the papers
are evaluated twice, provisions have been made on the first page itself for
the two evaluations. However, the third evaluation, which is necessary
only in a few cases, is provided for on the third page of the Answer
Booklet. He has drawn the attention of this Court to the Sample Answer
Booklet, which contains tkee parts - (l) details of the candidates, (ii) a
table for first evaluation (which is signed by valuer, chief examiner and
scrutinizer) and (iii) a similar table for the second evaluation. The first part
of the Arswer Booklet, i.e., details of the candidate, is removed prior to
sending for first evaluation. After obtaining the first evaluation and its
scrutiny, and prior to sending the paper for second evaluation, the portion

22
containirrg the reference to the first evaluation is removed. This is done to
ensure that the second evaluation is independent and uninfluenced by the
first evaluation. Third evaluation, when the di fference of more than 15%
mark b,:tween the Iirst two evaluations, is conducted by removing all
three parts. Therefore, there was absolutely no scope for any
at any stage of evaluation.

(e) Comn-rission awarded the same marks to certain candidates:
8. I 3. Learned Advocate General has submitted that it is quire

He has also referred ro rhe table provided at pg.926, vol.V showing thar so

27
many candidates got the similar marks. On the question of similar marks
secured by number of candidates, the selection procedure mentioned in
para 12.3 of recruitment Notification No.0212024 has been adverted to
which provides tie breaker principles at various levels. If the total marks of
two or more candidates are equal, then the local status of the candidates is
considered for ranking i.e-, candidates local to Telangana are given first
positions. If the total marks and local status of more than two candidates
are equal, then the date of birth of the candidates is taken forranking i.e.,
the elder candidate is given prior rank. If total marks, local status and date
of birth of nvo or more candidates are equal, then the ranks are awarded
based on the marks obtained by the candidates in Paper-I. If tie continues,
then highest marks in Paper-II would be given priority, even then if tie
continues then highest marks in Paper-III would be given priority, even
then if tie continues then highest marks in Paper-IV would be given
priority, even then if tie continues then highest marks in Paper-V would be
given priority. If then also two or more candidates are equal in all aspects,
the date of passing of the required qualifying examination will be taken
into account for consideration for ranking. If then also two or more
candidates are equal in all aspects as indicated above, the percentage of
marks in the required quali$ing examination will be taken for awarding
the rank i.e., the candidate having higher percentage in the required

28
qualifying examination is given prior rank. If then also two or more
candidates are equal in,all qsp,ggt$i?"t4eir:hieh€r qualification will be taken
into account. If then also,tw."g:"#fn*g&r-cgB4idates are equal in all aspects, "-,'::;.,r.

Reply submissions by the learned Advocate Ceneral:
10. [,earned Advocate General has submitted that during the course of
the lengthy hearings, almost every issue has been canvassed on behalf of
either p;rties and as such, any detailed reply is avoidable. He, however,
has cotmtered the pointed allegations by learned Senior counsel,
Mr. Surender Rao on the issue of addition of 60 posts to the earlier
advertisr:ment of 503 posts under Notification No.04/2022.|t is submitted
that this was the subject mafter of challenge in w.p.Nos.21239 and22320
of 2024, which were dismissed by the leamed u,rir court vide common
judgment *r"d 15.10.2024. He has referrecl ro paras 7 and, I0 of the
aforesaid judgment. It is also pointed out rhar the writ appeal Ncis.I lgg
and I 19) of 2024 preferred by the unsuccessfrrl writ petitioners was
dismissed on 18.10.2024. He has drawn the attenrion ro para 20 of the said
judgment. The Special Leave Petition (Civil) No.22790 of 2024 was also
dismissec[ on 06.12.2024. He has also brought ro the notice of this court
that some of the candidates challenged the Notification No.0212,024 in
: w.P.Nos.2@38 of 2024 and batch, which were dismissed on 06.12.2024.
He has also referred to the findings of the tearned writ court at para 23.
The Special Leave Petition (Civil) No.24t0 of 2025 prefened by the
unsuccessful writ petitioners was also dismissed on 03.02.2025. The
contentiort made on behalf of the writ petitioners on the issuance of

6l
Notification No.02l2024 after withdrawal of the Special Leave Petition
preferred by the Commission is therefore rnisplaced. The petitioners
carmot rake up the issue again. The Telangana State Public Service
Commission Regulations, 2014 provides for conduct of examinations by
the Commission. Para 3(ix)(d) of the Rules of Procedure clearly provide
that no case of revaluation would be permissible. Further, Rule 3(xi)(v) of
the Rules of Procedure gives enough flexibility to Commission to
prescribe the procedures which are incidental to the selection. The entire
thrust of the petitioners' case relates to the procedural aspects framed by

ANALYSIS ATYD DISCUSSION :
tl. We have considered the submissions of learned counsel for the
parties. We have also taken note of the copious materials placed on the
records ofwrit appeal No.1066 of 2025, which is the lead matter. We have
also taken note of the decisions relied upon by leamed counsel for the
parties. Some of them by both sides. The learned writ court in its
concluding para369 onwards felt that the Commission had not maintained
transparency, integrity, acted in biased manner and deviated from their

66
own rules in conduct of Group-l examination. Based upon the discussions
made and taking into account facts and circumstances referred to therein,
Final lr{arks List dated rl.B.za2s and the General Ranking List dated
30'03'2005 were set aside. The learned writ court,directed re-evaluation of
the ansrver scripts of Group-I Mains examination by applying moderation
method in rerms of Sanjay Singh (supra) manually and announce the
results- Based upon such results, respondents were directed to take steps to
fill up the said 563 posts. The learned writ court went on to issue
alternative direction that it is inclined to cancel the Group-r Mains
examination and direct for re-conduct under Notification No.02/2024.
dated 19.02.2024 for those who succeeded in the Frelims examination.

* I ) PROCEDLTRAL ASPECTST

.1.-, i
15. The findings on these issues have been assailed in the present
appeals by the Commission and the successful candidates. One cross-appeal h;m been filed by the writ petitioner, i.e., W.A.No.l470 of 2025
questioning the alternative direction for re-evaluation by following
moderati'cn on the lines of Sanjay Singh (supra). We proceed to examine
the correctness of the f,rndings of the learned writ court hereinafter. It is
also important to examine whether the alternative directions issued by the
leamed rvrit court were proper or mutually irreconcilable. Rival parties
have pla,:ed reliance on a number of decisions on the direction of re-a)
b)
c)
d)
e)
conduct of tr,""i?a#ffiiii.'rli!'p.op.. to refer to mS .re-gg{W.gnaeog in
Baishakhi Bhattacharj,jia (Chatterjee) by which the entire reoruitment
of Teachr:rs and non-teaching staff in the State of West Bengal numbering
12,946 were cancelled on account of systemic irregularities in the selection
process. 'the principles culled out from the precedents on the subject in the
case of [taishakhi Bhattacharyya (Chatterjee) (supra), at paras l8 and
19, are e>ltracted as under as they illuminate the course which the courts in
Judicial [leview have to follow.

73
"lS.lnVanshika Yadavv.Union of India [(202$ 9 SCC Z4]1.
this Court observed that a holistic view must be adopted by assessins
the e*tent of unfair means used and whether it is possibre to separare
the tainted candidates from the untainted ones. The court rnust ensure
that allesations of malpractice are substantiated and that the rnaterial
on record. including investigative reDorts. suDDorts this conclusion.
There rnust be at least some evidence for the court to reach such a
conclusion. However. the standard of evidence need not be undul;v
strict. Specifically. the material on record need not point to a sinsle.
definitire conclusion that malpractice occurred at a systemic level.
Neverrheless. there must be a real possibilitlr of systemic malaise. as
reflectcd in the material before thecourt.
19. The followine principles emerge from the aforesaid
discussion:
. W}ren An in<le-oth fae.tual innrrinr s svslernra
irregularities. such as malaise or fraud. that undermine the intesrity
of the entire selection orocess- the result should be cancelled in irs
entirety. However. if and when possible. seeremtion of tainted and
untainted candidates should be done in consonance with fairness and qqult
. The decision to carrcel the selection ez neasse must be based on
the satisfaction derived from sufficie,lrt rnaterial collected throueh a
fair and thoroueh investisation. It is not necessary for the material
collected to conclusivelv prove malpractice beyond a reasonable
doubt. The standard of evidence should be reasonable certairuv of
systemic malaise. The probability test is aoplicable.
. Desoite the inconvenience caused to untainted candidates.
when broad and deeo manipulation in tle selection onocess is proven.
due weiehtaee has to be givexr to maintaining the ourity of the
selection process.
. tndividual notice and hearing may not be necessary in all cases
for practical reasons when the facts establish that the entire selection
process is vitiated with illegalities at a large scale."
16. The principles summarised above require the Court to test whether
the conduct o[ the Group-I examination revealed systemic irregularities,
such as malaise or fraud that undermine integrity of the entire selection
process leading to the inevitable cancellation of the result in its entirety?
whether tainted and untainted candidates could be segregated in

71
consonance with fairness and equity? lt lays down that such decision to
cancel ttre selectioD. en masse must be based on the satisfaction derived
from strfficient material collected through a fair and thorough
investigation. It may not be necessary to conclusively prove rnalpractice
beyond a reasonable doubt, but the standard of evidence should be
reasonable certainty of systemic malaise. The probability test would apply.
The court also has to keep in mind that when broad and deep neanipulation
in the selection process is proven, due weightage has to be given to
maintain'ing the purity of the selection process, despite inconvenience to
the untainted candidates. Individual notice and hearing may not be
necessa4/ in all cases for practical reasons when the fact establishes that
the entire: selection process is vitiated with illegalities at a large scale. The
leamed unit corrt directed cancellation of entire Mains examination and
re-condu,:t under the same notification if the Frrst direction for re-evaluation through moderation on the lines of Sanjay Singh (supra) cou'ld
not be undertaken. [n view of the above directions of the learned writ
court, it is to be examined whether the Comrnission had adopted a method
of moderation which eschewed arbitrariness in evaluation and whether the
moderati,rn conformed to the yardsticks laid down by the apex court in a
series of decisions starting from Sanjay Singh (supra), on which parties
have plac'ed reliance.

18. In this background, we propose to deal with the findings of the
learned writ court on both aspects - Procedural Aspects and Method of
Evaluation, sequentially hereinafter.
19. The learned writ court held that the issuance of two hall tickets for
Group-l examination is suspect in view of the illegalities and inegularities
that have followed. Rules of Procedure did not require issuance of trvohall
tickets. That UPSC issues only one hall ticket. There was no material
placed regarding the selection to Group-I examination conducted in the
year 201 l, more over it was before bifurcation of the State. In this regard,
it is pertinent to mention that the Rules of Procedure and the Notification
No.02/2A24 do not bar issuance of two hall tickets. The candidates were
informed of the need to download a separate hall ticket for the Mains
examination through the Web Notes dated 16.08.2024 and 09.1,0.2024, i.e.,
before conduct of the Mains examination starting from 21.10.2024 to
27.10.2024. Both the Prelirns and Mains examination hall tickets
contained the respective examination centre codes. The Mains hall ticket
had reference to the Prelims hall ticket number as well. The issuance of
two hall tickets were followed in Group-I examination also under
Notification Nos.l51201I and l8l20ll as is evident from para 304 of the
impugned judgment. The appellants have also stated that for other
examination, such as for Civil Judges (Junior Division) examination,

84
Police Sub Inspector examination and Police Constable exarnination
different hall tickets were issued. In this background facts, it is to be seen
as to horv does the issuance of trvo hal[,1ickets prejudiced the case of the
writ petitioners? Whether issuanee of two hail tickets was conclusively
. ,.! ::..i: ,^t';,:'
shown to benefit any candidate or a group of candidates in the final result?
The leanred writ court has not recorded any such finding to that effect. The
writ petitioners have strongly urged that the issuance of separate hall ticket
for Mains examinations affected the integrity of the examinations. They
have hovvever failed to demonstrate as to in what manner it affected the
purity of'the examinations. Mere issuance of two hall tickets may have
caused srrspicion in the minds of the candidate, but unless any prejudice is
shown or proved by the probability test that candidates have specifically
. .ri. :: ,.. ..,t.trii:t!,' ,,. .
. t'
been favoured on account of, by the leamed writ
' '1!.,1.!
court were not justified.
20. On the second issue of allocation of examination centres in a biased
manner, the learned writ court has referred.to cente Nos.l8 and 19 where
female candidates were allocated. According to the learned writ court
proper r;rndomisation was not followed in the allotment of female
candidates to those centres. The Commission also did not give preference
to the physically challenged candidates, who were accommodated in the
second and third floor buildings. From the stand of the Commission, it is

85
apparent that allocation of test centres was done through computer
software in a random manner. Due to lack of toilets for men in centre
Nos.l8 and 19, these centres are allocated only for females. In any case,
mere allotment of female candidates to cenfie Nos.l8 and 19 for logistical
reasons could not lead to any inference of unfaimess or arbitrariness or
favouritism in conduct of the Group-I examination. Such allocation of two
centres to female candidates by the Commission cannot be a basis for
alleging a deliberate strategy which undermines the purity of the
examination process. No prejudice has been caused to any candidate
including the writ petitioners. The Commission has contended that no
disabled candidate had filed a complaint for their accommodation in the
second and third floor buildings. Only the unsuccessful candidates, who
were non-PwD candidates, had raked up this issue. It has also been stated
by the Commission that the test centres were increased from 45 to 46 to
accommodate candidates who were tarsferred after accommodating PwD
candidates from inaccessible centres. The increase in the number of test
centres again is purely in the domain of the Commission, a constitutional
body empowered to arrange the manner necessary for smooth conduct of
examinations. The petitioners cannot complain any prejudice on account of
allocation of examination centres nor have they been able to establish any
bias or malafide on facts in doing so.

86
2l - The leamed writ court has rendered a finding on the inconsistency
in the tor:al number of candidates who appeared for the Mains examination
by observing that the Commission was negligent in finding out correct
number of candidates who appeared. The Final Marks List had 21,085
candidat,:s which had increased from 20,161 published in Press Note dated
13.03.2025. The Commission in its common additional counter affidavit at
para 14 at page 912 explained the difference in the figures given in the
statistical summary datd27.10.2024, Press Note dated 13.03.2025 and the
Total Mark List dated 30.03.2025.It states that the figures were collected
from Chief Superintendents and were subject to minor variations as they
were rel,:ased 'on the qo'. A total of 2l,l l0 candidates had written the
examinal:ion. The figure in the Press Note dated 13.03.2025, i.e., 20161
excluded 924 candidates who did not qualiff in English and 25 whose
results were withheld due to High Court orders. The Total Marks List on
30.03.2025 reflected 21085 candidates which did not include 25
candidatcs, whose results were withheld. The variation in the summary
released on 27.10.2024 and the final count of candidates was only 17.
Leamed writ court observed that the Commission had used biometrics,
however biometrics were not used for real time attendance, but for
authentication of the identity of the candidate. The writ petitioners have
questiont:d the inconsistency in the flrgures of appearing candidates by the

87
Commission as a serious irregularity, which underrnines the sanctity of the
examinations. The variation in these figures does not lead to inesistible
conclusion that ghost candidates appeared and were declared successful,
neither are such instances shown by the petitioners. Therefore, the instant
issue (c) uncler 'Procedural Aspects' does not lead to any inference of
systemic irregularity in the conduct of the examination and that too in the
absence of proof of fabrication of results in favour of any candidate. Such
finding based on statistical assumption cannrct take the place of proof to
annul the exarnination itself.
22. On the fourth issue of multiple evaluation of answer scripts, the
learned writ court has commented that the Commission conducted a triple
evaluation method in which the third evaluation did not have barcodes and
bubbling, which increases the scope for manipulation. The Commission in
its affidavits stated that the third evaluation lvas conducted on a difference
of more than I 5oh in the evaluation of first and second evaluations. The
sample booklet was uploaded through Web Note, dated 16fi8.2024, more
than one month before conduct of the examination, also bore reference to
the third evaluation. It was contended that such procedure was valid in
2008 and 201I Group-l examinations in the united State of Andhra
Pradesh. The Commission also explained that due to barcode on stitched
part of the answer script, the third evaluation could be identified by the

88
same barcode given on the answer script. The evaluation and marking by
the two rlvaluators by bubbling of marks on the OMRs was done manually,
but that $o.L q.,f.gl.pecessarily lead to manipulation. The adoption of the
. .:, :
methg$lpp,gyplpgtloo by more than one examiner in every paper was in
fact a ,gefeg.uar, to ensure that hawk-dove effect between Iiberal and
conservativg expqiner is minimised to the maximum possible extent. In
any case, sqch a method was applied unifornnly for evaluation of all the
pap.ers,of a[.ggdidates as explained by the Commission. The petitioners
have on,ithe"oth.gr hand strenuously argued that the procedure of evaluation
gave apJgle sc€pe to identifu the candidate by the examiner. Moreover the

thirdevaluatgr did not have any barcode which could lead to manipulation

. :.i.,.; :.
that the procedure of multiple
hand were applied uniformly thereby

23. " Aller the evaluation by the first examiner and bubbling of marks
explainecl in detail by the Commission, the relevant part of the answer
sheet wari torn off from the remaining part at the perforation. The second
evaluator had no clue as to the first evaluation. Both strips containing the

89
marks by the evaluators were matched with the barcode on the ansrver
sheet later on. lf there was a difference of more than I5% between the first
and second evaluation, then such papers were given to the third evaluator.
The marks were given by the third evaluator on second page of the answer
sheet. They were matched with the barcode available on the first page.
Thus entire process has been graphically explained by the Commission in
its note referred to in the latter part of the judgment under the head
'Method of Evaluation'.
24. Any such procedure uniformly applied is in the iarger public
interest and introduces an element of fairness. Moroover, Rute 3(xi)(v) of
the Rules of Procedure confers a discretion upon the Commission to
evolve procedure for conducting the selection exercise. It supplements the
constitutional powers of the Commission for proper conduct of such an
examination. Writ petitioners have not been able to show that this
supplemental power in specific language or by necessary implication is in
conflict with the constitutional powers of the Commission to ensure a fair
conduct of the examination. Such provisions vesting discretion should not
be interpreted in a manner which would take away the discretion
contemplated under the Rule, rather it would be proper to interpret it in a
manner which furthers the object of such Rule.

90
25. The learned writ court has referred to paras 15.2 and 15.3 of the
on the issue of publication of provisional/total marks list. It
,Commission ought to have published the total rn*,,S.*,"of
with provisi onal paper-w i se marks. Provisional, .qlA-rl<s
could have enabled the candidates to compare their oUrn
marls with the marks of other candidates before seeking re-count. Thc
Commistion had wrongly sent individual subject marks to individual
logins. r\ccording to the learned writ court, the Commission failed to
fOlloU, tbre Rules of Procedure. Para I 5.2 has been repeatedly referred to by
lparnd r:ouusel for the writ petitioners also. It states that the total marks
,
.r$r,
ndi.W,!3ye0 on website and paper-wise marks of all candidates gao}p
respective logins. However, the observation o,L$e
marks sheets of all candidates
petitioners to compare their marks of
recounting, does not flow out of a reading of these clausps
together. A candidate is supposed to seek recounting of marks if, on his
assessment, ft", marks allotted in a particular paper is less than his
estimation. tt it neither conceivable nor permissible for a candidate to
know ab.1ut $re marks allotted to another candidate for seeking recounting
of his oum marks. It is to be noted that the Commission after recounting
publishedl the provisional marks list. The final marks list of every

9t
candidate was duly published on 30.03.2025. Though much arguments
lrave been addressed on the non-conlpliarrce of para 15.2 of the
Notification, but the petitioners have not been shown any prejudice or
suffered. Every candidate could access their paper-wise marks with the
respective logins for seeking recounting. It is not the case of the
petitioners, nor any material has been brought on record that the marks list
finally published on 30.03.2025, where fabricated in respect of few
candidates.
26. Paras 15.2 and 15.3 of the Notification construed harmoniously
means that candidates could access their paper-wise marks through their
respective logins. Any candidate could seek recounting of marks within 15
days after publication of the provisional marks list. Thereafter, the marks
list of the Mains examination (total marks) of all the candidates would be
displayed on the Commission's website. Thus read together it clearly
indicates that after recounting of marks, the marks list of Mains
examination (total marks) of all the candidates would be displayed on the
Commission's website. tt cannot be intended to mean that the Commission
was obliged to publish provisional marks list of all candidates for an
individual candidate to compare before seeking recounting of his own
marks. In this case, candidates have accessed their paper-wise
memorandum of marks through their respective logins and if dissatisfied,

92
have sought recounting. After the recounting process, the marks lists of the
Mains e;<amination have been displayed on the Commission's website on
30.03-2025- The findings of the leamed writ court on the alleged non-compliance of para 15.2 of the recruitment notification is, therefore,
misplaced.
27. T'he learned writ court has commented upon disclosure of the hall
ticket numbers of the unqualified candidates in the Mains Final Marks List
i.e., those who did not secure qualifying marks in English, still the other
papers rvere evaluated. It has observed that the Commission issued
Memorandum of Marks in other six subjects despite those candidates not
qualifuiug in English examination. As a matter of fact, no negative
inference, can be drawn if all the papers of those candidates who later
hmred out to have Dot secured qualiffing marks in English were evaluated
, i.i. .
simultaneously with other candidates. The Commission has sought to
justifr such act on the gmund that if on recounting such disqualified
candidatrs secured qualifuing marks in English, then the results could be
declared along with the other successful candidates without undertaking
the evaluation of the remaining six papers of those candidates by a later
exercise. AII the papers of all candidates were evaluated in the centralised
evaluation to ensure that evaluation of all such candidates took place by
same set of examiners in a centralised manner avoiding chances of

93
variability in evaluation ifdone later. In any case, the evaluation of all the
papers of all candidates together does not lead to any conclusion that such
candidates scord a march over more nreritorious candidates.
28. The discussion made hereinabove. lherefore, does not reveal any
systemic irregularity or fraud that undennines the integrity of the entire
selection process for its results to be cancelled in its entirety as per the
principle laid in Baishakhi Bhattacharyl'a (Chatterjee) (supra). It is to
be taken note of that there are no allegations o[ leakage olquestion papers,
rnass cheating or mass copying in the examination or answer keys being
available to the candidates before the exarnination. Learned counsels for
both parties have relied on a number of decisions on this issue including
that of Baishakhi Bhaftacharyya (Chatterjee) (supra).
29. The principles, on the basis of rvhich the cancellation of the
examination en tnasse can be allowed, have been summarised in the case
of Baishakhi Bhattacharyya (Chatterjee) (supra) relying upon several
pr€cedents. Based on those principles, we are of the considered opinion
tbatnime ofthe findings of the leamed writ court on the procedural aspects
establish any systemic irregularity, such as malice or fraud, which
undermines the integrity of the entire selection process calling for
canc€llation of the Mains examination. Not a single candidate is shown to
have unduly benefitted by any acts ofcheating or copying or on any count

94
formulated under procedural aspects by the learned writ court. The writ
petitioners have not been able to conclusively establish that the purity of
the seler:tion process was affected on account of any of these alleged
infirmiti,:s in the procedure followed by the Commission for conduct of
the exanrination as held by the learned writ court.
30. Ir the absence of any prejudice shown by the writ petitioners in the
entire pnrcedure for conduct of the examination, the writ petitioners cannot
be allow,:d to assail the conduct of the Mains examination.
31. As regards the increase of vacancies by adding new vacancies, the
leamed tvrit court has answered it in favour of the,Cornmission. Such plea
was rejet:ted. It has been brought to the notice of this court that challenge
to addition of 60 vacancies from the earlier notification dated 26.04.2022
had flailed. The matter was upheld till the apex court.
32. The decisions cited by the parties on these issues are being dealt
with hereinafter.
33. ln the case of Ashok Kumar Yadav (supra), it was held that the
suspicion cannot take place ofproof. The observations of the apex court, at
para 2l,u:re as under:
"... ... ... But suspicion camot take the place of proof and we
cannot strike down the selections made on the ground that the
e'raluation of the merits of the candidates in the viva voce
e:(amination might be a6itrary. It is necessary to point out that the
Court cannot sit in judgrnent over the rna*s awarded by the

95
interviewing bodies unless it is proved or obvious that the marking is
plainly and indubitably arbitrary or affected by oblique motives".
34. In the case of Tajvir Singh Sodhi (supra), at para 3l of the
judgment, the apex court laid down the principles of interference by courts
in the selection process for public employrnent
*3I. Before proceeding further, it is necessary to preface our
ju'dgment with the view that courts in lndia generally avoid
interfering in the selection process of public employment,
recognising the importance of maintaining the autonomy and
integrity of the selection process. The courts recognise that the
process of selection involves a high degree of expertise and
discretion and that it is not appropriate for courls to substitute their
judgment for that of a Selection Committee. It would be indeed,
treading on thin ice for us if rve were lo venture into reviewing the
decision of experts who form a part of a Selection Board. The law on
the scope and extent ofjudicial revieu, of a selection process and
results thereo{, may be understood on consideration of the following
case law."
35. It lala down that in respect of selection process of public
emplolment, tlre courts generally avoid interference, recognising the
importance of maintaining the autonomy and integrity of the selection
process. That the process of selection involves a high degree of expertise
and discretion. It is not appropriate for courts to substitute their judgment
for that of selection committee. It is not within the domain of the courts,
exercisiqg the power of judicial review, to enter into the merits of a
selection process, a task which is the prerogative of and is within the
expert domain of a selection committee, unless there are proven
allegations of malfeasance or violations of statutory rules or inherent
arbitrariness where courts can intervene.

96
36- lrr the case of Deependra Yadav (supra), the apex court referred to
the caser; of State of U.P. vs. Atul Kumar Dw,ivedi2e and Tajvir Singh
Sodhi (r;upra) on the application of -ra.rion/scaling of marks in a
l:.'t,'l
recruitrnr:nt process and as to when such an exercise would be permissible.
While upholding the exercise undertaken in adopting the process of
nonnalization was consistent with the requirement of law, the observations
of the ap,3x court at para 27 are as under:
the decisions made by expert bodies, including the
t'ublic Service Commissions, should not be lightly interfered with,
unlcss instances of arbitrary and malafide exercise of power are
rrrade out".
37. We now proceed to deal with the finding of the learned writ court
on five i:;sues under the heading 'Method of Evaluation'. The writ court
directed lhe Commission to undertake revaluation by adopting moderation
on the lines of Sanjay Singh (supra):

Commission awarded the sarne marks to certain candidates."

38. Tlte learned writ court has, on the selection of evaluators, referred
to the Prt:ss Note dated 13.03.2025 and observed that two retired faculty
mentbers were appointed as evaluators contrary to the assertion of the
Commissron. Dr. M.A.Malik was a private tutor, who also evaluated the
'n (:o:2) I I scc 578

97
answer scripts. He had written a book on Indian Economy for Mains
examination in the year 2024 and had guided students from R.C.Reddy
IAS Study Circle. The learned writ court, therefore, observed that the
Commission had not acted as per its proclamation in the Web Note that it
had engaged regular faculty from the University/Government Colleges
The petitioners have supported the findings and alleged that the
Commission acted contrary to its own professed standards and thereby
failed to maintain integrity of the evaluation process. [n this context, it is
to be noted that out of 35t evaluators engaged by the Commission, even
Dr. M.A.Malik against whom allegations have been made, was working in
Governrnent Degree College since 2010. He had written a book on lndian
Economy for the Mains examination. He has on affrdavit rebutted the
allegation that he took classes in R.C.Reddy IAS Study Circle for the last
fourteen years. In the scheme of evaluation, where the identity of the
candidate is totally anonymous and the marks are awarded by bubbling by
more than one set of evaluators, the process of evaluation of more than
2O,000.candidates could not be tainted on the basis of such an allegation
without any proof of a single instance, where the evaluation had resulted in
a particular candidate being favoured.
39. Mr. M.Surender Rao, learned Senior Counsel for the writ
petitioners had relied upon the case of D.Suvankar (supra) and submitted

98
that the selection of examiners or chief examiners should have been such
who are experts in the subject an(,1ealfy equipped for the job, otherwise
the evalrration would suffer. O+ tlUr l".q.t#,.ei,.$is Court finds substance in the
. .i ,..::i :
reply of the Commission that,inr..9qg$,r..9..--t9d of evaluation process, a totat
set of l4 subject experts were invo.lve$. in, evaluation of answer scripts of
seven papers o[ each candidate. Thus,, in all 28 different subject experts
evaluaterl answer booklets of seven p.apen of each candidate in two

Ii. Possibility of Decimals:
though the marks awarded by each subject expert in each paper
don't contain any fractions, the averaging of total marks secured in
t'ro evaluations in each paper of a candidate may result in fractional
values in the marks secured by each candidate in that particular
paper.

103
The following examples can be taken into consideration:
Example-A: Suppose a candidate secures IO0 marks in first
evaluation and 97 marks in second evaluation, then the marks
secured by that candidate would be the average of both evaluations
i.e., 98.5 marks (100 + 97 divided by 2).
Examole-B: Suppose a candidate secures I00 marks in first
evaluation and 74 marks in second evaluation, since the difference
between the first and the second evaluation is more than 159lo, the
paper of the said candidate will be sent for third evaluation. Suppose
if the candidate secures 87 marks in the third evafuation, then the
average of nearest marks in two evaluations will be taken. In this
example, the nearest marks are in first evaluation (100) and third
evaluation (87). Harce, the marks secured by that candidate would
be the average of first and third evaluation i.e., 93.5 marks (100 + 87
divided by 2).

intended to show transparency in the manner of conduct of the

48. In this regard, it is appropriate ro refer to decisions in Sanchit
Bansal (supra), Sunil Kumar (supra) and rajvir singh Sodhi (supra).
The apex court has consistently held that the court cannot sit as an expert
body over the decision of the Commission unless it is shown to be
arbitrary, suffering from mala fide or any violation of statutory rules.
under Rule 3(xi)(v) of the Rules of Procedure, the Comrnission has the
power to take decisions in respect of the selections made and to evolve the
procedure in any other matter incidental to the selection. The contention of
the writ petitioners that the introduction of the third evaluator was an
afterthought, thereby changing the rules of the game midway is also not
correct on the facts. The sample answer booklet uploaded through Web
Note dated rc.a8.2024 more than one monrh before the conduct of the
Mains examination also bears reference to the third evaluation. The
process of evaluation, in case of difference of more than 15% marks
awarded by the first and second evaluators, by the third evaluator, was
earlier also followed in 201I Group-l examination in the State of Andhra
Pradesh.,The entire argument of the writ petitionerc for the method of
moderation is based on the decisions rendered in the case of Sanjay Singh
(supra), Sujasha Mukherjee (supra), Manoj Kumar (supra), Disha
Panchal (supra) and Pranav Verma (supra).

49. The apex court in the case of Sanjay Singh (supra) laid down that
in order to avoid subjectivity of respective examiner in a large scale
examinat.ion where more than one examiner is involved to evaluate the : ..- i,1 :i;.r :i': . :.
answer r;cripts relating to a subject, resulting in hawk-dovQr,9..S.egt,,9.r

'examiner variability', a procedure is needed to be evolved. to ensure
uniformity inter se the examiners so that the effect of .lexaminer
subjectivity' or 'examiner variability' is minimised. This procedure is
known ars moderation. The apex court also observed that only certain
situation:; warrant adoption of scaling techniques. Scaling is employed
when carrdidates take examination in different subjects. Statistical experts
have evc,lved the scaling method, which places the scores from different
tests or test fornrs on to a common scale. The writ petitioners contended
that the rnethod of moderation in the case of Sanjay Singh (supra) was not
followed by the Commission. The petitioners also submitted that t[e
Commisr;ion had contradicted itself by stating that no moderation or
scaling vras required. In this regard, it is pertinent to deal with the decision
relied on this point by the rival parties.

The petitioners contended that the method of moderation laid down in

uniformity. Therefore, the learned writ court had directed moderation
rnethod to be followed as in Sanjay Singh (supra).
51. In the case of Manoj Kumar Yadav (supra) also relied upon by

result in'diiplacement of a number of selected candidates not before the
court and alteration of the merit list causing serious prejudice to those

upheld the finding of illegality in using scaling for all papers but moulded

r08
the relief to preserve the appointments and set aside the direction to
declare the results afresh-52. T,he case of Disha Panchat (supra) rgligd upQTliW.$*,roritioner's
. ' .... . '
counsel, Mr. Shadan Farasat, relates to "the ,Qgpdup.tro,,{,.Coq44on Law
Admission Test (CLAT),2018 entrance examiaation. The apex,court was
considering whether the entire examination should be cancelled due to

apex ccurt observed that any outright cancellation would cause

those candidates who suffered loss of time could be compensated, then

directcd thc authorities to compensate affected candidates by applying a

there are no such allegations of loss of examination time.
53. [n the case of Pranay Verma (supra), the apex court directed

petitioners, however, have not been able to show any undue strictness in

54. In this regard, the appellants had placed reliance upon the decision
of the apex court in Sunil Kumar (supra). In the said case, it was observed
that the requirement of, adoption of moderation to a particular kind of
examination and scaling to others, are at best, opinions, exercise of which
requires an in depth consideration of questions that are more suitable for
the experts in the field. An adoption of any singular process or by a
straitjacket formula cannot be ideal. The apex court further held that
Sanjay Singh (supra) did not decide that to an examination where the
papers are corunon, the system of moderation must be applied and to an
examiriation where the papers/subjects are different, scaling is the only
available option. Holding of public examinations involving wide and
varied subjectVdisciplines is a complex task which defies an instant
solution by adoption of any singular process or by a straitjacket fonnula.
Not only examiner variations and variation in arvard of marks in different
subjects are issues to be answered, there are several other questions that
also may require to be dealt with. Variation in the strictness of the
questions set in a multi-disciplinary examination format is one such fine
issue which was coincidentally noticed in Sanjay Singh (supra). It was
further held that a conscious choice of a discipline or a subject by a

55. The Commission in its reply through the common additional
counter erffidavit, have explained that the Comrnission had followed the

lll
moderation method for evaluation of the answer scripts as suggested by
the Hon'ble Supreme Court in Sanjay Singh (supra). The answer scripts
of the candidates were evaluated by two evaluators and further by another
evaluator (detailed in paras 48-64 of common additional counter affidavit).
The learned writ court misconstrued para 70 of Commission's common
additional counter affidavit where the Commission wrote
"scaling/moderation was not required" to be an admission on part of the
Commission that moderation was not applied by the Commission. The
Commission has submitted that the writ petitioners had loosely
interchanged scaling/moderation as one and the same term., and had
alleged that the Commission did not use scaling/moderation, to which the
Commission replied in the same terminology stating that scaling/
moderation was not required. All the candidates have written the same
subjects i.e., same seven papers and hence, when there was uniformity in
syllabus, moderation as a mode of evaluation and not scaling is applied.
Learned writ court, therefore, came to a flawed conclusion that the
Commission did not follow moderation method of evaluation and violated
the principles prescribed in Sanjay Singh (supra). Therefore the entire
results be declared invalid and re-evaluation be conducted by applying
moderation method in terms of Sanjay Singh (supra). Upon the survey of
the above decisions, this court is of the opinion that though the method of

tt2
moderation was approved in the case of Sanjay Singh (supra) with the
object tc ensure uniformity in the evaluation, to avoid 'examiner
subjectivity' or 'hawk-dove effect', but as held in the case of Sunil
Kumar (supra), it cannot be understood to lay down as an absolute
proposition that it is governed by adoption of any such singular process or
by straitracket formula. Holding of public examinations involving wide
and varied subjects/disciplines is a complex task. The standards of inter-subject evaluation of answer papers and issuance of appropriate directions
to evaluartors in different subjects are all relevant areas of consideration.
All such questions and several others should be left to the expert bodies in
the field, including the Public Service Commissions. Even if such bodies
have errt:d or have acted in less than a responsible manner in the past
cannot br: a reason for a free exercise of the judicial power, which by its
very nahrre is limited to instances of arbitrary or mala fide exercise of
power. The opinion of the apex court in Sunil Kumar (supra), at para 20,
is as undt:r:
"20. We cannot understand the law
-
to be imposing the
rr:quirement of adoption of moderation to a particular kind of
eramination and scaling to others. Both are, at best, opinions,
erercise of which requires an indepth consideration of questions that
are more suitable for the experts in the field. Holding of public
eraminations involving wide and varied subjectVdisciplines is a
c,)mplex task which defies an instant solution by adoption of any
singular process or by a straitjacket formula. Not only examiner
variations and variation in award of marks in diff€rent subjects are
issues to be answered, there are several other questions that also may
nquire to be dealt with. Variation in the strictness of the questions
sr:t in a multi-disciplinary examination format is one such fine issue

It3
lhat was coincidentally noticed in knjay Singh f&njay
Singh v. U.P. Public Service Commtssion, (2007) 3 SCC 720 :
(2007) I SCC (L&S) 8701 . A conscious choice of a discipline or a
subject by a candidate at the time of his entry to the Universiry
thereby restricting his choice of papers in a public examination; the
standards of inter-subject evaluation of aru;wer papers and issuance
of appropriate directions to evaluators in different subjects are all
relevant areas of consideration. All such questions and, may be,
several others not identified herein are required to be considered,
which questions, by their very nature should b€ l€ft to the expert
bodies in the field, including, the Public Service Commissions. The
fact that such bodies including the Commissions have erred or have
actd in less than a responsible nmnner in the past cannot be a reason
for a free exercise of the judicial power which by its very nature will
have to be understood to be, norrrally, limited to imtances of
arbitrary or mala fide exercise of power."
56. The rnethod adopted by the Commission to avoid hawk-dove effect
or examiner variability, as explained in their note, and the affidavits,
therefore, rule out any instarrce of arbitrariness or malafide or violation of
statutory rules. The method of evaluation employed by the Commission
through subject experts in each paper in the first and second evaluation,
and then the award of marks on average of total marks in the first and
second evaluation of each answer .booklet in itself is to ensure thar
'examiner variability' or 'hawk-dove effect' is minimised. Moreover,
when there is a difference of lSYo or more between the total marks of
answer booklets of each paper in the first and second evaluation, such
answer booklets are sent for third evaluation to be evaluated by different
set of relevant subject experts by repeating the same process followecl in
the frrst and second round of evaluations including affixing the signature
of the subject experts, Chief Examiner and Scrutinizer. The ultimate effect

tt4
of adopting this method is to minimise the 'examiner variability'. It
applies uniformly to all candidates. Holding of public examinations
involvin;g wide qubjeqts/disciplines is a complex task. The
j' :' .:
method adopted, being an expert body in the field
should not be mhde open to speculative challenges. In Sunil Kumar
(supra), the apex court observed that the decision in Sanjay Singh {supra)
cannot be understood to lay down as absolute proposition that it is
goveme<l by adoption of any singular process or by a straitjacket formula.
The writ petitioners have in their submissions suggested alternative
methods, which the Commission should have adopted by for conducting
moderation. The Court in Judicial Review, however, cannot sit as an
:f i... :l
expert body over the $gc_i1ion 9f the Commission on the manner and
,iila:
,.
:: i .t:
method jrn which ev.4lqgtion is to.be made, unless it suffers fr.om patent i . .: i,:,'r*l:,'l ;i, t ,-.
arbitrarirress, malafide.or any violation of statutory rules.
57 . \&'e are, therefore, unable to agree with the findings of the learned
writ corrrt that the procedure of evaluation suffered warranting
re-evaluartion by following the method laid down in the case of Sanjay
Singh (supra).
58. Rule 3(xi)(v) of the Rules of Procedures confers wide discretion to
prescribe procedure in matters incidental to selection. Rule 3(xiXv) is
quoted hr:reunder:

115
"3(xi) A decision as to the following shall be taken by the
Commission in respect of all selections rnade by them.
(i) to (iv) xxx
(v) any orher marter incidental to the selection."
59. The examining body like the Public Service Commission has wide
powers under Article 320(3) to take decisions on the manner and method
in which the evaluatiron has to be mad€J If such procedure or method of
evaluation has been uniformly applied, the court should be loathe to sit as
an expert body over the decision of the Commission unless it is shown to
be arbitrary, suffering from malafide or in violation of statutory rules. The
learned rvrit court, however, failed to take into account the salutary
principles goveming exercise ofjudicial review in such matters.
60. During the course of submissions, learned Senior Counsel flor the
writ petitioners, Mr. G.Vidya Sagar has drawn the attention of the Court to
terms and conditions of the recruitrnent notification and contended that any
deviation thereflrom by the Commissionrwas arbifrary He relied upon the
case of Babu Verghese (supra) and Ambay Cements (supra) to submit
that if the manner of doing a particular act is prescribed under any statute
or for that matter the recruitment notification, the Commission could only
undertake the evaluation process prescribed therein or not at all. Where a
power is given to do a certain thing in a certain way, the thing must be
done in that way or not at all.

il6
61. It is im-portant to refer to Article 320 of the Constitution
whereunder the.Union and the State Public Service Comnnissions are
to conduct examinations for appointments to the
the States respectively. Article 320(3) provides
Commission shatl be consulted on the principles to
be folloiued in'rhaking appointments to civil services and posts and in
making promotions and transfers from one service to another,
62. The Commission has framed Rules of Procedure for conduct of
examinations. The Commission, under the extant rules, is empowered to
take decisions in respect of selections made by them enumerated under
Rule 3(xi) and also dhy other matter incidental to the selection. Therefore
t6*-itrion deviated from its professed terms
Notification in the
'in law. Iflthe Commission
and method of evaluation, which is inciderital=tb
the selec,rion and if such method does not suffer from arbitrariness or mala
, fidn ancl is applied uniformly to all candidates, no irregularity in the
manner and method of evaluation process can be alleged.
63. The case of Veerendna Kumar Gautam (supra) relied upon by
learned Slenior Counsel, Ms. B.Rachna Reddy is on the proposition that if
the proc,:ss of selection is found to be tainted, whatever had flowed

tt7
consequent thereto must also fall along with the process of selection. The
appointment of selected candidates being subject to outcome of these
appeals will have to go once the findings of the learned writ court both on
Procedural Aspects and the Method of Evaluation are upheld on the basis
of the materials placed by the rival parties.
64. However, in the considered opinion of this court, such a result
would follow only if the conduct of the Group-I examination by the
Commission is found to be suffering from the systemic irregularity or
malice or fraud that undermines the integrity of the entire selection
process. We are afraid that no such conclusion can be arrived at on the
basis of the allegations made by the writ petitioners without any material
to prove any such malpractice in the conduct of the examination, even
standards of probability test. The writ petitioners have failed to show any
instance of mass cheating or mass copying or leakage of question papers or
tutoring of, candidate during the examination to substantiate such
allegation.

cp-{!i$trC{[Thgldegree college faculty in the State ofTelangana teach bofh
in Englir,h and Telugu medium to the students of graduation course. The
evahiator.s ,were, therefore, competent to evaluate Group-I Mains
sxemin?4on in both languages. The Commission in its reply has
catqorically .refuted any such widespread neglect of the candidates
unitin, g in Telugu medium. Such a finding o[' the learned writ court,
thenefore; appears to be more on assurnption than on any factual basis
supPgft€d.b ary specific instances.
.6.f. f,lae ,learned writ court has also gone to observe that for so4e
number of qualified candidates rvere higher. There.g4gs
'.,,"
proportion of female candidates selected in centre Nosrt$
and 19 v:rsus the remaining centres. -[his finding is not reflective of actual
success mte of other centres. Centre Nos. I 8 and 19 which accommodated
721 ard776 female candidates, had the success rate o( 5.41o/o and 4.12%0.
Fourteen other centres had a success rate of 4yo and six
centres had a success rate of 5Vo. Centre No.9 had a success rate of 8.70Yo.
Cenhe Nb.8 had a success rate of 6.25%o and Centre No.l2 had success
rate of 6.100/o. As such, the inference based on statistics cannot be the

il9
basis to suspect any malfeasance in those centres specifically when none
from those centres ever made any complaint of cheating, mass copying or
tutoring of any particular candidate or female candidates of those centres
having been favoured. It is quite statistically possible that certain centres
may have higher proportion of well performing candidates. It was also
pointed out to the learned writ court, relying upon a press release ofUPSC
dated 28.Q7 .2023 that in open competition, more candidates getting
qualified from some centres is usual. The purity of the examination
process cannot be doubted on the basis of such statistics which even if
taken on face value do not show any stark or exceptional results compared
to other centres.
67. The leamed writ court, therefore, fell in error in holding that the
presence of successful candidates from specific centres had any basis to
affect ttre integrity of the examination process. It is worth reiterating that
there was no complaint of allegation of cheating, mass copying, Ieak of
question papers or tutoring of any candidates in any centre in lhe conduct
of the examination spread over 46 centres. It is important to once again
remind ourselves that any interference in the conduct of examination of
such a nature by the constitutional body, like the Public Service
Commission should not be made in the absence of proven mala fides,

120
arbitrariness or systemic irregularity as has been held in the case of
Baishak hi Bhattacharyya (Chatterjee) (supra).
68. Lastly, on the issue of award of sanre marks to certain candidates,
the leanred writ court has opined that several candidates were awarded
same marks, i.e.,719 in number and especially those who had adjacent hall
ticket numbers. In this regard, it is important to take note that in the Mains
examination, 2l,l l0 candidates had appeared and 12,622 candidates had
obtained qualifying marks. The marks ranged between 270 for
SC/ST/PwD candidates to 550. Therefore, it is not improbable for multiple
candidatr:s securing the same total marks, when the range of marks is
rurrrower. Further, the Commission had demonstrated that even though the
candidatrr having adjacent hall tickets had secured same total marks, their
marks fur individual subjects were different. The examinations were
conducted under CC TV Surveillance, wherein no cheating or irregularities
were alleged or noticed. Even in the case of UPSC Examinations, 2024,
out of I ()09 candidates, 969 candidates shared a total with at least one
. other carrdidate. The recruitment notification in a case of same marks
between several candidates has under para 12.3 provided multi-levelled tie
breaker system based on status of local status, date of birth, higher marks
in papeni I to V and marks in English, date of passing of required
qualifuinl; examination and marks in higher qualification. The ranking

l2l
even amongst the candidates having the same marks is dependent upon a
numhr of factom including securing lhe marks in dillerent papers. As
such, the assumption made by the leamed writ court at best could be a case
of suspicion, but in the absence of any proven malfeasance or
demonstrable acts of wrong doing it could not be the basis to undermine
the entire recruitment process. The findings of the leamed writ court,
therefore, on both the procedural aspects and method ofevaluation that the
Commission failed to maintain transparency, integrity and violated the
rules do not appear to be based on sound reasoning and established from
the materials on record. The petitioners did not allege mala fides or fraud.
The materials on record could not lead to any conclusion that recruifircnt
process suffered from grave and systemic irregularity.
69. In Maharashtra State Board of Secondary and lligher
Secondary Education (supra), the apex cou( has held that the court
should be extremely reluctant to substitute its own views in relation to
academic matters in preference to those formulated by professional men
possessing technical expertise and rich experience. Parla 29 of the =
aforesaid judgme t is extracted as under:
"29. Far ftom advancing public interest and fair play to the other
candidates in general, any such interpretation of the legal position
would be wholly defeasive of the same. As has been repeatedly
pointed out by this Court, the Coun should be extremely reluctant to
substitute its own views as to what is wise, prudent and proper in
relation to academic matters in prcference to those formulated by
professional men possessing technical cxperlise and rich experience

122
,)f actual day-to-day working of educational institutions and the
rlepanments controlling rhem. It will be wholly wrong for the Court
to rnake a pedantic and purely idealistic approach to the problerns of
lhis nature, isolated from the actual realities and grass root problems
involved in the working of the system and unmindful of the
(:onsequences which would emanate if a purely idealistic view as
opposed to a pragmatic one were to be propounded. It is equally
imporlant that the Coun should also, as far as possible, avoid any
rlecision or interpretation of a statutory provision, rule or byeJaw
rvhich would bring about rhe result of rendering the system
tmworkable in practice. lt is unfo(unate that this principle has not
been adequately kept in mind by the High Coud while deciding the
instant case."
70. In the absence of mala fides, proven systemic irregularity or
violation of any statutory rule, therefore the direction to undertake
re-evaluation with moderation in the lines of Sanjay Singh (supra) or to
re-conduct Mains examination does not sustain on scrutiny of the materials
placed on record.
7l . In a case where the method of evaluation has been uniformly
applied in larger public interest, no candidate can be prejudicially affecred.
The dire:ction of the leamed writ court to re-evaluate the Mains
examination papers with moderation in the .lines of Sanjay Singh (supra)
also does not sustain on accormt of the reasons recorded hereinabove based
on a detailed analysis of the method of evaluation employed by the
Commission and after due consideration of the submissions of the parties
on this iss;ue.
72. The integrity of the examination conducted by a constitutional
body, likt: the Public Service Commission, cannot be made suspect on the

t23
basis of surmises and unsubstantiated allegations. The direction of the
leamed writ court for re-conduct of the examination as an altemative is
therefore unsustainable.
73. Therefore, the impugned cornmon judgrnent passed in
W.PNds.l1439 of 2025 and batch, dated 09.09.2025 is set aside and the
writ appeal Nos.l066, 1054, 106?,1068, t069, 1070, 1071, 1072, t073,
1074, LWs, 1076, rc77, 1078, 1079, I 080 and I 086 of 2025 arc allowed.
74. On the basis of the elaborate reasons recorded hereinabove, writ
appeal No.l470 of ?fl25 preferred by rhe peririoners in W.p.lrlo.l 2206 of
2025 insiiting upon re-conduct of the Mains examinarions, is frt to be
disEissed-Miscellareous applications pending, if any. shall stand closed.
Acco,rdingly, the writ appeal No. 1470 o12025 is dismissed.
